=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a binary string s of length n and an integer numOps.
You are allowed to perform the following operation on s at most numOps times:

Select any index i (where 0 <= i < n) and flip s[i]. If s[i] == '1', change s[i] to '0' and vice versa.

You need to minimize the length of the longest substring of s such that all the characters in the substring are identical.
Return the minimum length after the operations.
 
Example 1:

Input: s = "000001", numOps = 1
Output: 2
Explanation: 
By changing s[2] to '1', s becomes "001001". The longest substrings with identical characters are s[0..1] and s[3..4].

Example 2:

Input: s = "0000", numOps = 2
Output: 1
Explanation: 
By changing s[0] and s[2] to '1', s becomes "1010".

Example 3:

Input: s = "0101", numOps = 0
Output: 1

 
Constraints:

1 <= n == s.length <= 1000
s consists only of '0' and '1'.
0 <= numOps <= n

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def minLength(self, s: str, numOps: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's craft code:

class Solution:
    def minLength(self, s: str, numOps: int) -> int:
        n = len(s)
        INF = 10**9

        def can(k: int) -> bool:
            # dp0[r], dp1[r]: min flips for processed prefix ending with r copies of 0/1
            dp0 = [INF] * (k + 1)
            dp1 = [INF] * (k + 1)
            if s[0] == '0':
                dp0[1] = 0
                dp1[1] = 1
            else:
                dp0[1] = 1
                dp1[1] = 0

            for ch in s[1:]:
                cost0 = 0 if ch == '0' else 1
                cost1 = 0 if ch == '1' else 1

                min_dp0 = min(dp0)
                min_dp1 = min(dp1)

                ndp0 = [INF] * (k + 1)
                ndp1 = [INF] * (k + 1)

                # start a new run of 0 after a run of 1
                ndp0[1] = min_dp1 + cost0
                # start a new run of 1 after a run of 0
                ndp1[1] = min_dp0 + cost1

                # extend existing runs
                # r from 1 to k-1
                for r in range(1, k):
                    if dp0[r] < INF:
                        ndp0[r + 1] = dp0[r] + cost0
                    if dp1[r] < INF:
                        ndp1[r + 1] = dp1[r] + cost1

                dp0, dp1 = ndp0, ndp1

            return min(min(dp0), min(dp1)) <= numOps

        lo, hi = 1, n
        while lo < hi:
            mid = (lo + hi) // 2
            if can(mid):
                hi = mid
            else:
                lo = mid + 1
        return lo

```
class Solution:
    def minimumLength(self, s: str, numOps: int) -> int:
        def check(m):
            cnt=0
            i=0
            while i<n:
                j=i
                while j<n and s[j]==s[i]: j+=1
                length=j-i
                if length>m:
                    cnt += length//(m+1)
                i=j
            return cnt <= numOps
```

Now, let's ensure code syntax correct:
```
class Solution:
    def minLength(self, s: str, numOps: int) -> int:
        n = len(s)
        if numOps >= n // 2:
            return 1
        INF = 10**9

        def can(k: int) -> bool:
            dp0 = [INF] * (k + 1)
            dp1 = [INF] * (k + 1)
            if s[0] == '0':
                dp0[1] = 0
                dp1[1] = 1
            else:
                dp0[1] = 1
                dp1[1] = 0

            for ch in s[1:]:
                cost0 = 0 if ch == '0' else 1
                cost1 = 0 if ch == '1' else 1

                min0 = min(dp0)
                min1 = min(dp1)

                ndp0 = [INF] * (k + 1)
                ndp1 = [INF] * (k + 1)

                ndp0[1] = min1 + cost0
                ndp1[1] = min0 + cost1

                for r in range(1, k):
                    if dp0[r] != INF:
                        ndp0[r + 1] = dp0[r] + cost0
                    if dp1[r] != INF:
                        ndp1[r + 1] = dp1[r] + cost1

                dp0, dp1 = ndp0, ndp1

            return min(min(dp0), min(dp1)) <= numOps

        lo, hi = 1, n
        while lo < hi:
            mid = (lo + hi) // 2
            if can(mid):
                hi = mid
            else:
                lo = mid + 1
        return lo
```

Let's examine the DP transition more formally:
`dp0[r]` = min flips for prefix processed ending with exactly r zeros. `dp1[r]` similarly.
For next char `ch`:
- If we set it to 0:
   - If previous char was 1, new run of zeros length1. Cost cost0. We can choose any previous state ending with 1, so min_dp1.
   - If previous char was 0 and previous run length r (1..k-1), new run length r+1. Cost dp0[r]+cost0.
- If set to 1 analogous.
We don't allow previous char 0 with run length k to extend to k+1. Good.
At the end, any state valid. Good.

In each iteration, we need min(dp0) and min(dp1) before update. We could compute them at start of iteration by scanning dp arrays. But we also scan dp arrays in extension loop. We can combine: compute min while extending? But extension loop only r=1..k-1, and min needs include dp[r] for r=1..k. We can compute min0 = min(dp0) and min1 = min(dp1) separately. Or maintain after each iteration:
- min0_new = min(ndp0), min1_new = min(ndp1). But to compute ndp0[1] we need min1_old. So we can maintain old mins. At end compute new mins by scanning ndp? That still O(k). But we can compute new mins during construction:
```
min0_new = INF; min1_new = INF
# ndp0[1] = min1_old + cost0; update min0_new
# ndp1[1] = min0_old + cost1; update min1_new
for r...
    val = dp0[r]+cost0; ndp0[r+1]=val; if val<min0_new: min0_new=val
...
min0_old, min1_old = min0_new, min1_new
```
This avoids min(dp) scan. But still extension loop. Could reduce constant. Not necessary but easy. Let's consider implementing this optimized version to be robust.

Pseudo:
```
def can(k):
    dp0 = [INF]*(k+1); dp1=[INF]*(k+1)
    if s[0]=='0':
        dp0[1]=0; dp1[1]=1
    else:
        dp0[1]=1; dp1[1]=0
    min0 = min(dp0); min1 = min(dp1) # or set manually
    for i in range(1,n):
        ch=s[i]
        cost0 = 0 if ch=='0' else 1
        cost1 = 0 if ch=='1' else 1
        ndp0=[INF]*(k+1); ndp1=[INF]*(k+1)
        new_min0 = INF; new_min1 = INF
        # start new runs
        val = min1 + cost0
        ndp0[1] = val
        if val < new_min0: new_min0 = val
        val = min0 + cost1
        ndp1[1] = val
        if val < new_min1: new_min1 = val
        for r in range(1,k):
            if dp0[r] != INF:
                val = dp0[r] + cost0
                ndp0[r+1] = val
                if val < new_min0: new_min0 = val
            if dp1[r] != INF:
                val = dp1[r] + cost1
                ndp1[r+1] = val
                if val < new_min1: new_min1 = val
        dp0, dp1 = ndp0, ndp1
        min0, min1 = new_min0, new_min1
    return min(min0, min1) <= numOps
```
Need initialize min0,min1 after first char:
```
min0 = dp0[1] # 0 or1
min1 = dp1[1]
```
This avoids min(dp) scan. But code slightly more complex. Current code simpler. n=1000, no need. But maybe if constraints were 10^5, this optimized version still O(n*k) not enough. So no.

Final code:
```python
class Solution:
    def minLength(self, s: str, numOps: int) -> int:
        n = len(s)
        if numOps >= n // 2:
            return 1

        INF = 10**9

        def can(k: int) -> bool:
            dp0 = [INF] * (k + 1)
            dp1 = [INF] * (k + 1)

            if s[0] == '0':
                dp0[1] = 0
                dp1[1] = 1
            else:
                dp0[1] = 1
                dp1[1] = 0

            for i in range(1, n):
                ch = s[i]
                cost0 = 0 if ch == '0' else 1
                cost1 = 0 if ch == '1' else 1

                min0 = min(dp0)
                min1 = min(dp1)

                ndp0 = [INF] * (k + 1)
                ndp1 = [INF] * (k + 1)

                ndp0[1] = min1 + cost0
                ndp1[1] = min0 + cost1

                for r in range(1, k):
                    if dp0[r] != INF:
                        ndp0[r + 1] = dp0[r] + cost0
                    if dp1[r] != INF:
                        ndp1[r + 1] = dp1[r] + cost1

                dp0, dp1 = ndp0, ndp1

            return min(min(dp0), min(dp1)) <= numOps

        lo, hi = 1, n
        while lo < hi:
            mid = (lo + hi) // 2
            if can(mid):
                hi = mid
            else:
                lo = mid + 1
        return lo
```
Let's double-check early return with n=2, numOps=1. n//2=1, return1. Suppose s="00", answer1. yes. Suppose s="01", answer1. yes.
n=3, numOps=1, return1. Suppose s="000", flip middle ->010 max1. yes. Suppose s="001", flip first ->101 max1. yes. Suppose s="011", flip last ->010 max1. yes. Suppose s="010", already. yes.
n=4, numOps=2, return1. Suppose s="0000", flip positions1,3 ->0101 or1010 max1. yes. Suppose s="0101", already. yes. Suppose s="0011", flip pos2? 0101 diff2, yes. Good.
n=5, numOps=2, return1. Suppose s="00000", flip pos1,3 ->01010 max1. yes. Suppose s="00111", pattern01010 diff? pos3 1 vs0, pos4 1 vs1? Let's compute min maybe2. yes. Good.

Now, let's think about if early return could be wrong for n=1, numOps=0 because n//2=0 and 0>=0 true; answer1. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "numOps" maybe negative? constraints no.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "can(k)" if k is large and `min(dp0)` includes dp0[0]=INF but dp0[1] maybe INF? no.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "for i in range(1, n)" and n maybe 1; if early return not triggered? For n=1, early return always triggered because numOps >=0. So can not called. If we removed early return, loop range(1,1) empty and return min(dp0),dp1. For s[0]='0', dp0[1]=0, dp1[1]=1, min0. Good. But with early return, okay.
